# NCM Race Week Radio --- Character & Authoring Bible

> **STATUS: CURRENT** - authority for: *Race Week Live: the authored cast, editorial rules and every on-air line (the corpus `ncm/radio` compiles)* - imported verbatim from the owner's authoring bible 2026-10-08 (L348; expansion passes I-II, sections 176-249, imported the same day). Only this banner was added. Station architecture: [59_NCM_RADIO.md](../59_NCM_RADIO.md).

**Status:** Living writer's document\
**Purpose:** Define the deterministic, authored Race Week radio cast,
their relationships, voices, recurring scenarios, and rules for how the
show shapes the player's experience.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## 1. Core premise

This is not a literal shared broadcast that every NCM player receives
identically. It is the player's authored Race Week radio experience,
presented through the fiction and production language of a live Night
City talk-radio show.

The show covers Race Week as a whole: races, schedules, standings,
machines, engineering, incidents, economics, teams, R&D, city policy,
Moody events, and the changing physical and social condition of Night
City.

Most of the time it behaves convincingly like radio. It can preview
cards, argue about drivers, recap results, go trackside, bring in
technical voices, cover city consequences, and temporarily switch into
live race coverage when the player enters an event.

At rare, carefully chosen moments, the fiction may bend and the hosts
may address the player directly.

The commentary is **deterministic and authored**. Simulation facts
select authored beats. We do not attempt to generate natural
conversation by randomly assembling sentence fragments. A beat may be a
complete 20--90 second conversation with zero, one, or several
controlled simulation substitutions.

**Simulation determines what happened. Writers determine what it means
to these characters.**

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## 2. Production identity

### Working station/show identity

**Working title:** *Race Week Live*\
This is intentionally provisional. The cast should be designed before
locking the final show/station name.

### Advertising

The show is proudly **ad-free during Race Week**.

This can itself become part of the show's personality. Rather than
stopping for fake commercials, the hosts can occasionally joke about the
absence of sponsors, refuse an implied break, or use the space where a
commercial would normally occur for another segment.

The important production consequence is that the show does not need
artificial ad breaks to structure time. Stingers, music beds, segment
handoffs, race starts, news updates, and moments of silence provide the
punctuation.

### Primary format

Two equal co-hosts are the spine.

They are not interchangeable announcers. They have different histories,
instincts, tastes, blind spots, senses of humor, and attitudes toward
Race Week.

Neither is "the database voice."

Facts belong to NCM. Opinions belong to the characters.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# 3. Primary hosts

## Mara Velez

**Age:** 38\
**From:** Heywood, Night City\
**Role:** Co-host; former local motorsport reporter and street-racing
obsessive\
**Core orientation:** People, competition, consequences

### Background

Mara grew up close enough to Night City's informal racing culture that
cars were never abstract machinery to her. They were social objects:
somebody saved for that car, somebody wrecked that car, somebody lied
about what was under the hood, somebody's brother stopped coming home
after one particular night.

She never became a serious driver. She did enough amateur driving to
understand that she was much better at watching races than surviving
them.

Her career developed through local reporting, pirate sports coverage,
race recaps, interviews, and being the person who would actually remain
at a terrible event after everyone else had decided the story was over.

Race Week is the assignment she always wanted and increasingly suspects
may be a terrible idea.

### Why she cares about Race Week

Mara believes Night City desperately needs things that allow people to
care about one another for reasons other than gangs, corporations,
violence, or survival.

She wants Race Week to work.

This matters enormously on Day 3.

When she begins questioning whether it can continue, it should hurt a
little because the listener has heard her spend Day 1 defending it.

She is not anti-racing when things become dangerous. She is a racing
person confronting what racing is doing to her city.

### What she notices

-   drivers and recurring competitors
-   standings and personal arcs
-   rivalries that are actually evidenced
-   underdogs
-   hometown/location texture
-   human consequences of incidents
-   when a result means more than its finishing position
-   when somebody is becoming a recognizable Race Week figure
-   when the city appears to be paying too high a price
-   hypocrisy from officials or teams
-   the emotional absurdity of machines doing things machines should not
    be doing

### Biases

Mara has a weakness for underdogs and stubborn privateers.

She distrusts a favorite who appears inevitable.

She is more willing than her co-host to say that something has crossed a
line.

She can also become attached to a narrative and occasionally has to be
corrected when the numbers do not support it.

### Cadence

Conversational, fast when excited, willing to interrupt herself.

Uses contractions freely.

Can say things such as:

-   "was gonna be"
-   "I mean"
-   "come on"
-   "no, seriously"
-   "that's the thing"
-   "I don't know what you want me to tell you"

She is articulate without sounding written.

When something is genuinely serious, her language gets simpler rather
than more dramatic.

### Secret/backstory reservoir

Do not expose these immediately.

-   Mara lost someone she knew in an illegal race years before Race
    Week. Exact relationship remains to be decided.
-   She has therefore spent years simultaneously loving racing and
    distrusting the romantic language people use around danger.
-   She initially refuses to tell this story on air because she does not
    want her coverage reduced to trauma.
-   A sufficiently severe Race Week may eventually make the history
    relevant.
-   She has driven more aggressively than she admits and is not morally
    clean about the culture she critiques.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## Julian "Jules" Mercer

**Age:** 44\
**From:** North Oak upbringing, long professional history around City
Center and Watson paddocks/shops\
**Role:** Co-host; former mechanic, occasional club racer, technical
broadcaster\
**Core orientation:** Machines, strategy, systems, absurd possibility

### Background

Jules came into racing through machinery rather than journalism.

He has worked around shops, private teams, wealthy owners, terrible
owners, brilliant mechanics, improvised race cars, expensive failures,
and machines that had no business being competitive but somehow were.

He raced enough to have opinions and not enough to mythologize himself
as a former star.

He moved into broadcasting because he could explain why a car was
behaving strangely without turning the explanation into a lecture.

He is fascinated by engineering solutions that initially look stupid.

### Why he cares about Race Week

Jules sees Race Week as the largest uncontrolled engineering experiment
Night City has ever accidentally agreed to host.

Every race produces evidence.

Every failure teaches somebody something.

Every ridiculous machine that works annoys a person who thought they
understood the rules.

He begins Race Week more comfortable with danger than Mara is. Over
time, however, he can become disturbed when engineering capability
outruns the infrastructure required to contain its consequences.

### What he notices

-   machines
-   setup choices
-   engineering changes that are publicly supportable
-   fuel and tires
-   reliability
-   race strategy
-   unusual performance
-   regulations
-   technical loopholes
-   R&D
-   economic cost of machinery
-   whether a machine's observed behavior matches expectations
-   new capabilities demonstrated physically

### Biases

Jules loves inelegant solutions that work.

He is suspicious of expensive machinery being treated as inherently
superior.

He is more tolerant of technical loopholes than Mara.

He dislikes rules that prohibit something merely because officials did
not anticipate it.

He becomes much less amused when a loophole creates danger that the city
cannot physically manage.

### Cadence

Dryer than Mara, but not a deadpan machine.

Often waits a beat before delivering the part of the sentence that
changes its meaning.

Likes answering questions with qualified answers.

Common rhythms:

-   "Technically..."
-   "That is one interpretation."
-   "I didn't say it was a good idea."
-   "It worked."
-   "Those are different questions."
-   "I'd like to see it come back before we call this a success."

He is comfortable letting Mara fill silence and then puncturing the
conversation with one sentence.

### Secret/backstory reservoir

-   Jules has participated in engineering work that crossed regulatory
    lines.
-   One old project succeeded technically and failed socially or
    physically.
-   He knows what it feels like to be proud of a machine immediately
    before realizing what that machine enables.
-   He is initially evasive about why certain engineering failures make
    him stop joking.
-   His history should eventually complicate any simple "Mara cares
    about safety, Jules cares about speed" division.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# 4. The chemistry

Mara and Jules are **equal co-hosts**.

Neither is permanently play-by-play and neither is permanently color
commentary.

Their central disagreement:

**Mara asks what Race Week is doing to people and Night City. Jules asks
what Race Week is teaching people how to do.**

Both questions become increasingly dangerous as the Week progresses.

They genuinely like each other. Their disagreements are not manufactured
cable-news fights.

They know one another's recurring arguments well enough to anticipate
them.

Example rhythm:

**MARA:** Don't.

**JULES:** I didn't say anything.

**MARA:** You were about to call this an engineering success.

**JULES:** It is an engineering success.

**MARA:** There is a car on fire.

**JULES:** Those facts are not mutually exclusive.

This is the tonal center of the show.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# 5. Supporting cast / recurring segments

The supporting cast should be smaller than the number of possible
topics. One character can own a recognizable domain and return often
enough that the player knows who has entered before hearing the name.

## Imani Cross --- News Desk

**Age:** 33\
**From:** City Center\
**Role:** Short factual news updates and public-information
interruptions\
**Personality:** Controlled, quick, occasionally unable to hide
disbelief

Imani is the closest thing the show has to a conventional broadcaster.

She handles facts that should feel institutionally reported rather than
conversationally inferred: published results, explicit closures or
restrictions when supported, official Race Week changes, team
announcements, major publicly reportable incidents.

Her restraint makes it meaningful when something actually rattles her.

**Stinger:** clean, short newsroom ident.

She should not become the show's exposition machine.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## Tomas "Tommy" Rhee --- Trackside

**Age:** 29\
**From:** Japantown\
**Role:** Starts, grids, finishes, immediate physical race observations\
**Personality:** Enthusiastic, slightly breathless, loves being
physically near the machinery

Tommy gets the worst assignment from a hearing-preservation perspective
and considers this a privilege.

He is useful for transitions into player races because the soundscape
itself can move from studio to track.

He does not know hidden race state. He reports what the authorized live
surface can establish.

He is especially good for:

-   grid atmosphere
-   start
-   obvious position changes
-   visible incidents
-   finish
-   immediate post-finish chaos

**Acoustic world:** engines, crowd/road noise where supportable as
production ambience, radio compression, environmental space.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## Nia Okafor --- Garage / Technical

**Age:** 41\
**From:** Santo Domingo\
**Role:** Engineering, machinery, R&D, technical interpretation\
**Personality:** Precise, amused by bad assumptions, refuses to pretend
certainty

Nia is not "Jules but more technical."

Jules thinks like a racer/mechanic. Nia thinks like an engineer
examining evidence.

Her most important phrase may effectively be:

**"We don't know yet."**

She distinguishes observed capability from inferred mechanism.

If a Galena stays airborne for nine seconds, she can discuss the
observation without magically knowing the hidden component
configuration.

She becomes extremely important as engineering begins producing
unfamiliar capabilities.

**Acoustic world:** workshop/paddock, restrained tool/mechanical
texture.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## Darnell Price --- Street Desk

**Age:** 52\
**From:** Watson\
**Role:** Ground-level Night City perspective\
**Personality:** Funny, irritated, practical, unimpressed by motorsport
mythology

Darnell exists to keep the show from believing its own Race Week
propaganda.

He cares about whether you can get through an intersection, whether
barriers appeared overnight, whether a neighborhood is sick of noise,
whether some absurd race has made ordinary movement impossible.

He does not represent "public opinion." He represents **Darnell**.

That distinction is critical.

His observations must be grounded in actual world/policy/event state
when he makes factual claims.

**Acoustic world:** clearly recorded somewhere in Night City rather than
a studio.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## Celeste Ward --- Money / Paddock Business

**Age:** 36\
**From:** Corpo Plaza\
**Role:** purses, markets, teams, contracts, sponsors, economic
consequences\
**Personality:** Cheerfully ruthless about money

Celeste treats motorsport economics as another form of racing.

She can explain why a purse matters, why a machine loss matters, why a
transfer is interesting, or why an announced sponsorship changes
expectations without inventing private solvency.

She is useful because the economic story of Race Week should eventually
become as legible as the sporting one.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# 6. Direct player address

**UNRESOLVED DESIGN QUESTION.**

The show normally behaves as a personalized but diegetic-ish Race Week
broadcast.

Rare events may allow the fiction to bend and acknowledge the listener
directly.

The first Deep crossing is the leading candidate.

Important distinction:

-   first Deep crossing in world history
-   player's first Deep crossing
-   first Deep crossing after Deep is publicly established
-   routine return to Deep

These should not necessarily receive the same treatment.

### Current preferred principle

**Direct address is a scarce dramatic resource.**

The first use should feel startling because the show has spent hours
behaving as though the player is merely one subject among many.

Potential first-Deep rhythm:

**MARA:** Hold on.

**JULES:** What?

**MARA:** Can they hear us?

**JULES:** Who?

**MARA:** The driver.

**JULES:** ...Probably.

**MARA:** All right. You. Yes, you. We're talking to you.

From there, the show can acknowledge what the player demonstrably knows
without automatically making that knowledge public to all of Night City.

Whether direct address becomes an occasional permanent capability after
this moment or remains a one-time rupture is deliberately undecided.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# 7. Race Week macro arc

The show is not a fixed script where every Week deteriorates
identically. These are authoring climates selected by actual state.

## Day 1 --- Festival

Race Week begins as something people want.

Topics:

-   opening excitement
-   card previews
-   favorites
-   qualifying
-   machines
-   predictions
-   newcomers
-   first results
-   traffic/rules
-   early standings
-   technical curiosity
-   jokes about the scale of the event

The hosts are energized.

Mara wants this to work.

Jules wants to see what people build.

## Day 2 --- Memory

Yesterday now exists.

Topics:

-   standings taking shape
-   repeat winners/losers
-   grudges and evidenced recurring matchups
-   damaged/repaired machinery
-   engineering reactions
-   repeat incident locations
-   changed policy
-   Moody consequences
-   unexpected contenders
-   fatigue
-   money already changing hands
-   whether early predictions survived contact with reality

The show begins referring backward constantly.

## Day 3 --- Consequence

Race Week has accumulated weight.

Possible topics:

-   severe incidents
-   safety pressure
-   restrictions
-   infrastructure strain
-   damaged locations
-   machines disappearing from fields
-   championship stakes
-   desperate engineering
-   economic winners and losers
-   whether continuing is defensible
-   racers becoming more committed while the city becomes less tolerant

The hosts need not agree that Race Week should end.

A severe state may produce dialogue such as:

**MARA:** I really thought this was gonna be a total hit. I did. And
now... I mean, look at what's happened since yesterday.

**JULES:** I'm looking.

**MARA:** People are getting hurt. Machines are getting destroyed. Half
the city feels like it's being held together with barriers.

**JULES:** That's not half the city.

**MARA:** You know what I mean.

**JULES:** I do.

The factual specifics used in a final beat must be supported by
simulation state.

## Days 4--7 --- Prolonged Race Week

Never treat these days as software anomalies or "bonus days."

Inside this continuity, Race Week is simply still happening.

The city and competitors have had longer to adapt, accumulate damage,
change policy, develop standings, spend money, repair machines, and
normalize things that seemed extraordinary on Day 1.

The corpus can be smaller than D1--D3 but must feel like genuine later
days rather than repeated Day 3 surprise.

A seven-day Week should feel **lived in**.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## Moody

Every Moody event needs:

1.  factual announcement/acknowledgment if public
2.  hosts' immediate interpretation
3.  later callback if its consequences remain relevant
4.  optional disagreement between hosts
5.  variants by Week day/state

Moody should feel like Race Week's weather system for policy/culture
rather than a game modifier announcement.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# 9. Authoring rules

1.  **Write beats, not soup.** Complete conversations are preferred over
    assembling many independent sentences.

2.  **Variables are seasoning, not structure.** A beat may contain
    `{DRIVER}`, `{LOCATION}`, `{COUNT}`, `{MARGIN}`, etc., but the
    conversation should have been written to work as a conversation.

3.  **Silence is authored.** Not every event receives commentary.

4.  **Characters may be wrong in opinion, never wrong in supplied
    fact.** They may predict badly, disagree, underestimate somebody,
    dislike a strategy, or misunderstand significance when evidence
    permits uncertainty.

5.  **Do not manufacture public consensus.** A host's opinion is a
    host's opinion. Darnell's opinion is Darnell's opinion.

6.  **Do not expose hidden simulation state as character knowledge.**

7.  **Let callbacks create humanity.** A line from D1 can become funny,
    painful, or embarrassing on D3.

8.  **Repetition is acceptable when human.** Radio hosts repeat
    subjects. The goal is not zero repetition; it is avoiding obvious
    mechanical repetition.

9.  **Not every line needs perfect grammar.** Interruptions, fragments,
    contractions, self-correction, unfinished thoughts, "like," "I
    mean," and conversational overlap are valid.

10. **Seriousness reduces ornament.** Catastrophic events should often
    make the characters simpler and quieter rather than more theatrical.

11. **The show covers racing first.** Race Week is the subject.
    Extraordinary systems enter because they affect Race Week, not
    because the show exists to advertise NCM's scope.

12. **The simulation supplies nouns and verbs. The cast supplies
    judgment.**

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# 10. Audio-production identities

A segment change should be audible before its first word.

### Main studio

Warm, intimate talk-radio sound. Two people who sound physically
co-present rather than separately generated voices.

### News

Short clean stinger. More controlled acoustic treatment.

### Trackside

Environmental race/road sound and communications texture.

### Garage

Workshop/paddock acoustic identity.

### Street

Clearly recorded in-world at a location, with environmental texture.

### Money/Paddock

Clean but distinct from News; financial/sports-desk energy rather than
emergency bulletin.

Do not rely on voice selection alone to distinguish segments.

# 12. Day 1, Hour 0 --- The Fixed Opening and the First Long-Form Beats

## Authoring decision: length is cheap

The radio is allowed to **talk**.

Do not optimize authored beats toward one- or two-sentence efficiency.
This is a talk show whose job is partly to fill time. A useful
deterministic beat may run for several minutes if the subject supports
it.

Long beats reduce the pressure to create thousands of tiny
interchangeable fragments. They also allow the hosts to sound like
people who develop an idea, interrupt it, wander slightly, return to it,
disagree, tell a story, and eventually land somewhere.

A beat can therefore be:

-   a short exchange
-   a two-minute argument
-   a host monologue with occasional interruptions
-   a long interview or reminiscence
-   a multi-stage conversation with simulation substitutions
-   a serious memorial/aftermath segment
-   a callback to something said hours or days earlier

The desired breadth comes from **many scenario families and variants**,
not from chopping every thought into interchangeable sentence soup.

Repeated long beats are acceptable at low frequency. They should be
numerous enough that the station develops variety, while simulation
state increasingly changes which branches remain eligible.

## The Groundhog-Day anchor

Race Week should have one recognizable fixed opening.

The exact wording can recur at the beginning of every fresh Race Week.
That repetition is useful. It becomes an acoustic landmark: the listener
hears the familiar opening and immediately understands that Race Week
has begun again.

The hosts themselves do not treat the recurrence as strange.

### Working fixed opening --- Version A

**MARA:** And here we go. Welcome to *Race Week Live*. Night City's very
first Race Week is officially underway. Seven days of cars, speed,
prizes, rivalries, records, bad decisions, good decisions that are
probably gonna look bad at first, and more racing than anybody in this
city has ever tried to put in one place. I'm Mara Velez.

**JULES:** Julian Mercer.

**MARA:** Jules, we have been talking about this thing for months.

**JULES:** You have been talking about this thing for months.

**MARA:** You were here.

**JULES:** I was frequently here while you talked about it.

**MARA:** And now it's real. Entries are in, grids are filling up, the
first events are coming, and for the next seven days Night City is doing
something either completely brilliant or completely irresponsible.

**JULES:** Those are not mutually exclusive.

**MARA:** That is why I keep you around. Race Week starts now.

This is a working draft, not sacred copy.

The opening should establish:

-   show identity
-   Mara
-   Jules
-   nominal seven-day Race Week
-   racing across Night City
-   excitement
-   slight awareness that the premise is ridiculous
-   affection rather than cynicism
-   room for later irony

It should **not** front-load a systems tutorial.

After this fixed anchor, the show immediately diverges into eligible
Hour-0 beats.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## Hour-0 editorial climate

At Hour 0, the simulation has produced very little Race Week history.

That is not a content problem.

It gives the hosts a unique set of subjects that become less appropriate
later:

-   What is Race Week?
-   Why is Night City doing this?
-   Why do people race?
-   Why would anyone enter?
-   What are Mara and Jules expecting?
-   What are they afraid of?
-   What do they hope happens?
-   What does a successful Week look like?
-   Which disciplines/machines/events are coming?
-   What did preparation look like?
-   What do they think Night City will tolerate?
-   What does seven days of continuous motorsport even mean?
-   Who benefits?
-   Who thinks this is stupid?
-   How much money is moving?
-   What happens to ordinary roads?
-   What kinds of people sign up?
-   What does it mean that essentially any eligible machine can become a
    racing machine?
-   Can a city this chaotic actually sustain organized racing?
-   What will they be embarrassed to have predicted by Day 3?

Hour 0 should feel almost Olympic: intense attention on tiny early
details because nothing has happened yet.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## Beat D1-H0-01 --- "Why would anybody do this?"

**Eligibility:** D1, very early Week, before substantial
results/incidents exist.

**Purpose:** State the absurd premise from inside the world so the
player does not have to. Let Jules defend racing without pretending it
is rational.

**MARA:** Okay, I want to start with the question that everybody who
does not care about racing has been asking me for the last month.

**JULES:** Why.

**MARA:** That's it. Why. Why are we doing this?

**JULES:** That's a broad question.

**MARA:** No, seriously. We live in Night City. The roads are already
full. Half the people driving them think a lane marker is a personal
insult. And now somebody has looked at all of that and said, you know
what this needs? Timing gates.

**JULES:** Correct.

**MARA:** And you agree with them.

**JULES:** Completely.

**MARA:** Explain yourself.

**JULES:** People have been racing in this city for as long as there
have been two people with cars and one of them thought theirs was
faster.

**MARA:** That is not an argument for letting them do it everywhere.

**JULES:** I'm not finished.

**MARA:** Sorry.

**JULES:** No, you're not.

**MARA:** No.

**JULES:** The racing already exists. The cars already exist. The bad
decisions definitely already exist. Race Week is the part where somebody
finally puts a clock on it. There are routes. There are grids. There are
results. Somebody wins, somebody loses, and tomorrow everybody gets to
argue about why.

**MARA:** So your defense of Race Week is that Night City was already
irresponsible.

**JULES:** My defense is that competition turns chaos into evidence.

**MARA:** That is the most Jules Mercer sentence you've said today.

**JULES:** It's early.

**MARA:** All right. Evidence of what?

**JULES:** Who's fast. Which cars work. Which ones don't. Which drivers
can actually finish what they start. Which ridiculous idea somebody
built in a garage at three in the morning turns out not to be
ridiculous. You can advertise a machine all you want. You can tell me
what it should do. Put twelve of them on a grid and I'll tell you what
they actually do.

**MARA:** And this is why you're excited.

**JULES:** This is why I'm here.

**MARA:** I'm excited because I want to know who we're gonna know by the
end of this. There are names on these entry lists right now that mean
absolutely nothing to most of the city. A week from now, maybe one of
them means something.

**JULES:** Assuming we make it a week.

**MARA:** Jules.

**JULES:** What?

**MARA:** Hour one.

**JULES:** You asked.

Not every opening beat should be bombastic.

This beat is designed to become painful or funny in retrospect without
explicitly foreshadowing a predetermined catastrophe.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# 13. Scenario architecture: long-form variants

For major scenario families, plan multiple **whole-conversation
variants**, not merely alternate lines.

Example:

`SEVERE_INCIDENT_FIRST_OF_WEEK`

could eventually have:

-   Variant A: Mara leads, shaken; Jules factual
-   Variant B: Jules witnessed technical cause; Mara focuses consequence
-   Variant C: cause unknown; both refuse speculation
-   Variant D: incident follows earlier joking conversation, creating
    tonal whiplash
-   Variant E: incident involves a familiar competitor
-   Variant F: incident occurs during already strained Week
-   Variant G: player caused/participated, with knowledge boundary
    respected

Each can run several minutes.

The same is true for:

-   Moody events
-   first win
-   repeated winner
-   upset
-   catastrophic quickhack
-   major economic shift
-   first credible flight
-   first Deep crossing
-   Week continuation under severe strain
-   unusually safe Week
-   Day 7

The tree becomes broad through **authored situations**, while each
branch is allowed to be deep.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# 14. Tone rule: do not pre-write the tragedy

Race Week often descends into severe strain, but Day 1 dialogue must not
behave as though the writers know that this particular Week will become
catastrophic.

Lines such as "right now it's clean" are valid because they describe the
present and can acquire irony later.

Lines that effectively promise a future disaster should be avoided
unless the simulation has already supplied evidence.

A remarkably safe, successful seven-day Race Week must remain
narratively possible.

That means the same Hour-0 optimism can pay off in two radically
different ways:

-   by Day 3 it sounds heartbreakingly naive
-   by Day 7 it sounds like the beginning of something Night City
    actually managed to sustain

Both outcomes should feel authored, not exceptional error cases.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# 15. Day 1, Hour 0 --- The First Five Races

## Editorial correction: Day 1 believes in Race Week

Mara and Jules do **not** enter Race Week waiting for the disaster.

They know racing is dangerous in the ordinary sense. Cars crash, drivers
make mistakes, machines fail, and rules matter. But on Day 1 they
sincerely believe the organized Race Week project is a great idea and
that Night City can pull it off.

They are not secretly winking at the causal loop.

The writers know what Race Week can become. The hosts do not.

Therefore:

-   A normal clean race is **not surprising**.
-   A successful start is **not evidence that disaster has temporarily
    failed to happen**.
-   Normal retirements, mistakes, and contact are racing, not omens.
-   Early results are exciting because they create sporting history.
-   A genuinely bad event is shocking because it collides with the
    hosts' optimistic model of Race Week.
-   The first serious cracks force them to revise beliefs they actually
    held.
-   Never write backwards from a likely Day 3 state into Day 1 dialogue.

Their Day-1 baseline is closer to an Olympic opening day: huge sporting
event, enormous enthusiasm, curiosity about competitors, fascination
with firsts, and confidence that the machinery around the event exists
for a reason.

The causal loop belongs to NCM's architecture. **It does not leak into
their psychology.**

------------------------------------------------------------------------

\[Pause.\]

**MARA:** I don't really know what to say yet. I was sitting here five
minutes ago talking about how incredible it was that we finally had the

**JULES:** We don't know `{UNRESOLVED_FACT}` yet.

**JULES:** That's it for now.

\[Pause.\]

**MARA:** I still want to believe this can be what everybody built it to

**JULES:** Yeah.

**MARA:** Imani is going to stay with what is actually confirmed. We're

The shock comes from contradiction, not foreknowledge.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## If a severe event has already occurred

Do **not** use the cheerful Race 2--5 beats unchanged merely because
their ordinal matches.

A genuinely severe event creates a new authored emotional branch. The
hosts may try to return to sport, may be relieved by ordinary racing,
may remain distracted, or may question continuation depending on actual
severity and official response.

Crucially, this branch begins **after the event**.

Nothing before the event foreshadows that it was coming.

# 16. Opening-race branch dimensions

The first-five-race corpus should eventually receive a variance pass
across whole conversations.

Useful dimensions include:

### Result shape

-   favorite wins
-   upset
-   repeat winner
-   five different winners
-   close margin
-   dominant margin
-   penalty-adjusted result
-   DNF-heavy result
-   unusually clean result
-   player wins
-   NPC wins
-   player does not enter

### Safety history

-   no meaningful incidents yet
-   one recent incident
-   severe event very recent
-   several minor events accumulating
-   policy already changed
-   race occurs after a quiet recovery period

### Player relevance

-   player spectating/listening only
-   player entered
-   player wins
-   player podiums
-   player DNFs
-   player causes known incident
-   player is victim of known incident
-   player is irrelevant to result

### Competitive history

-   no repeat names yet
-   early favorite performing as expected
-   favorite struggling
-   surprise competitor emerging
-   same two drivers repeatedly adjacent
-   first standings lead change

### Editorial posture

-   exuberant
-   curious
-   analytical
-   relieved
-   uneasy
-   shaken
-   cautiously recovering

These are selectors for authored variants, not instructions to
interpolate emotional adjectives into a generic script.

By the end of Race 5, ordinal-specific coverage yields to the broader
salience system. Race 6 can receive ten minutes if something important
happens; Race 14 can receive thirty seconds if nothing does.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# 17. Day 1 After the Opening --- Race Week Condition Lanes

## Scale correction: do not write the Week as a small recurring cast

NCM has **hundreds of drivers** across many races, series, classes, and
schedules.

The radio must reflect that scale.

The normal Day-1 assumption is **not** that the same handful of drivers
keep appearing in consecutive races. Consecutive events will often
involve completely different fields and different winners.

Therefore:

-   Do not spend primary authoring effort on
    `RACE_2_WINNER == RACE_1_WINNER`.
-   Repeat winners across relevant events remain a valid rare/salient
    beat, but they are not the spine.
-   The show should constantly introduce new names.
-   A driver can matter for thirty seconds, win a race, and disappear
    from the show until much later.
-   Recurrence becomes interesting precisely because the population is
    large.
-   Standings, series, classes, teams, and championship contexts should
    prevent the show from pretending every race belongs to one universal
    small field.
-   Early Day 1 is a torrent of introductions. Race Week feels bigger
    than the hosts can completely hold in their heads.

Likewise, ordinary Day-1 radio should remain focused on Race Week and
Night City. References to external terrestrial circuits such as Laguna
Seca, Monza, and other reachable worlds/venues should wait until the
player's epistemic state and Deep history make those places appropriate.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## Race density

Race Week may produce races on the order of every several minutes,
sometimes stacked and sometimes separated by quiet periods.

The show cannot and should not give every race a bespoke ordinal script.

After the specially authored opening races, race coverage is selected
from:

1.  the current race facts,
2.  the race's salience,
3.  the current Day-1 Race Week condition lane,
4.  recent events,
5.  player relevance,
6.  series/championship relevance,
7.  whether the show has recently covered too many similar races.

The hosts are passengers to the simulation. They do not decide whether
Race Week continues. They report, interpret, argue, celebrate, worry,
and occasionally question what they are watching while the event
continues around them.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# 18. Day-1 Condition Rubric

This is the major variance axis for Day-1 race commentary.

The condition lane is an **editorial interpretation of canonical recent
state**, not a literal public numeric meter.

It should be sticky enough that one minor incident does not swing the
entire show's personality, but responsive enough that a cluster of
serious events changes the atmosphere.

A useful conceptual input set includes:

-   severity and recency of incidents
-   verified casualties/consequences where such facts actually exist
-   number and severity of race disruptions
-   policy/safety response
-   infrastructure response
-   frequency of serious failures
-   recent recovery/quiet periods
-   whether organizers have explicitly changed rules or infrastructure
-   whether races are still proceeding normally
-   current Race Week curve/policy facts that are actually permissible
    for editorial selection

Do not narrate the hidden score.

## Lane A --- EXPECTED / GOOD RACE WEEK

**This is the baseline.**

Do **not** say that Race Week is going smoothly.

Do **not** congratulate the city for avoiding catastrophe.

Do **not** continually discuss safety.

This is simply Race Week as Mara and Jules expected it to be.

The show talks about:

-   races
-   winners
-   qualifying
-   machines
-   standings
-   series
-   classes
-   predictions
-   arguments
-   funny results
-   engineering
-   money
-   teams
-   the schedule
-   what is coming next
-   who they are beginning to recognize

Routine contact, DNFs, penalties, and mechanical failures are
motorsport.

### Example: ordinary finish

**TOMMY:** Gonzalez across the line first! `{P2}` behind, `{P3}` takes
third.

**MARA:** What a race from Gonzalez.

**JULES:** That last section did it.

**MARA:** And there's another new name for the board. I swear every time
I look down we've got somebody else to learn.

**JULES:** You wanted a big field.

**MARA:** I love the big field. I'm just gonna need a bigger notebook.

Nothing about this beat says "nobody died." That thought is not present.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## Lane B --- SLIGHTLY BELOW EXPECTATIONS / BAD TASTE

Race Week is still fundamentally fun and credible, but something has put
a little grit in the broadcast.

Perhaps there have been several ugly contacts, a concerning incident, a
troublesome location, repeated disruptions, or an official response.

The hosts are not asking whether Race Week should exist.

The bad taste **percolates**.

They may:

-   mention a recent incident during a new race
-   note new barriers or an announced response
-   be slightly more attentive to a difficult section
-   sound relieved when an affected driver/machine returns, without
    treating normal racing itself as surprising
-   ask whether organizers have fixed a specific problem
-   briefly acknowledge uncertainty and then return to sport

### Example: race at a location with recent trouble

**TOMMY:** We're at `{LOCATION}` for `{EVENT_NAME}`. Field is coming
together now.

**MARA:** And this is the first race back through here since
`{RECENT_SUPPORTED_EVENT}`.

**JULES:** They've made `{SUPPORTED_INFRASTRUCTURE_OR_POLICY_CHANGE}`
since then.

**MARA:** Good. I want to see the racing. I also don't want to spend the
next hour talking about that corner again.

**JULES:** I suspect the drivers agree.

**MARA:** All right. New race. New field. Let's see what they do with
it.

The concern is attached to an actual recent fact. It is not generalized
dread.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## Lane C --- SUBPAR RACE WEEK

By now the hosts cannot treat the problems as isolated texture.

Race Week is still happening. They still care about the racing. They
still celebrate excellent performances.

But the event is falling short of what they believed it would be.

Typical tone:

-   disappointed
-   frustrated
-   conflicted
-   organizers increasingly discussed
-   safety/infrastructure questions recur
-   ordinary race coverage carries context from the broader Week
-   Mara may explicitly say that this is not what she thought they were
    signing up to cover
-   Jules may focus on whether concrete interventions are working
-   both still want Race Week to recover

### Example: pre-race

**MARA:** They are going ahead with `{EVENT_NAME}`.

**JULES:** They are.

**MARA:** `{SUPPORTED_RESPONSE}` is in place. We've had
`{SUPPORTED_RECENT_CONTEXT}`. So, yeah, I have questions.

**JULES:** So do I.

**MARA:** But there are drivers on the grid, the event is live, and
we're going to cover the race that's actually happening.

**JULES:** `{TECHNICAL_OR_ROUTE_FACT}` is what I'm watching.

**MARA:** I'm watching whether we can get through one of these without
adding another problem to the list.

**JULES:** Fair.

**MARA:** Tommy, what have you got?

### Example: good race inside a subpar Week

**TOMMY:** `{WINNER}` takes it!

**MARA:** Great drive. Seriously. Great drive.

**JULES:** `{SUPPORTED_PERFORMANCE_FACT}`.

**MARA:** And I don't want the rest of what's happening around Race Week
to swallow that. `{WINNER}` earned this result. That's a race worth
talking about.

This is important: a bad Week does not make every sporting
accomplishment sound funereal.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## Lane D --- BAD RACE WEEK

The event has become genuinely troubling on Day 1.

The hosts' original assumptions have been damaged.

Race Week continues because the simulation/organizers continue it, not
because Mara and Jules have decided it is acceptable.

Typical tone:

-   disbelief that events are still proceeding
-   frequent official updates
-   explicit questions about continuation
-   safety instructions when actually authorized
-   less joking
-   races framed inside the unresolved situation
-   victories still acknowledged, but the broadcast cannot pretend the
    surrounding context is normal
-   anger becomes possible
-   silence becomes more common

### Example: race still proceeding

**MARA:** `{EVENT_NAME}` is going ahead.

**JULES:** Yeah.

**MARA:** We have been in contact with Race Week officials. The latest
confirmed information is `{SUPPORTED_OFFICIAL_STATUS}`. That's what we
know.

**JULES:** The field is on the grid.

**MARA:** And we're coming to you live from `{LOCATION}`. I want
everybody in this race over the line safely. That's where I'm at right
now.

**JULES:** Same.

**MARA:** Tommy, take us down there.

### Example: finish

**TOMMY:** `{WINNER}` crosses first. `{P2}` second. `{P3}` third.

**MARA:** Congratulations to `{WINNER}`. I mean that.

**JULES:** Good race from them.

**MARA:** It was. And we're going to talk about it. But we're also still
waiting for `{SUPPORTED_PENDING_OFFICIAL_ISSUE}`. As soon as Imani has
something confirmed, we will bring it to you.

The result is real. The crisis is also real. Neither erases the other.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## Lane E --- CATACLYSMIC DAY 1

This should be rare.

Race Week has gone catastrophically worse than Mara and Jules believed
possible this early.

The show may now sound partially like an emergency broadcast while still
being forced by reality to cover races that continue.

Do not make them cool about it.

Do not make them secretly thrilled by the drama.

Do not allow banter to bounce back automatically after a race trigger.

Possible characteristics:

-   "Why is this still going?" becomes a legitimate question.
-   The hosts repeatedly seek official confirmation.
-   They may tell people to stay away from a location or stay off
    streets **only if an authorized public instruction exists**.
-   Race starts can feel surreal.
-   A winner can cross the line into silence.
-   Mara may openly question whether the show should still be
    celebrating.
-   Jules may stop defending the experiment and focus almost entirely on
    concrete mitigation.
-   Imani's role grows.
-   Darnell/Street may become more important if grounded observations
    exist.
-   Trackside may become uncomfortable rather than festive.
-   The show can spend long periods on one event.

### Example: another race is somehow still happening

**MARA:** They did not cancel `{EVENT_NAME}`.

**JULES:** No.

**MARA:** So we're coming to you from `{LOCATION}`. The race is going
ahead. `{SUPPORTED_OFFICIAL_STATUS}` remains the official position.

**JULES:** `{SUPPORTED_SAFETY_OR_INFRASTRUCTURE_CHANGE}` is in place.

**MARA:** I want to be really clear about where we are. We started today
celebrating this thing. I was celebrating it. I thought this was gonna
be incredible for the city. And we've had
`{SUPPORTED_CATASTROPHIC_CONTEXT}`. There are a lot of people asking
what happens now. We are asking too.

**JULES:** We don't have an answer yet.

**MARA:** No. We don't. The drivers are on the grid anyway.

\[Pause.\]

**MARA:** Tommy?

**TOMMY:** Yeah. I'm here.

**MARA:** Take it when you're ready.

### Example: finish under catastrophic conditions

**TOMMY:** `{WINNER}` is across. That's the win.

\[Short pause.\]

**MARA:** All right. `{WINNER}` wins `{EVENT_NAME}`.

**JULES:** `{SUPPORTED_RESULT_FACT}`.

**MARA:** Congratulations to them. I wish I had more for that result
right now. I don't.

**JULES:** That's okay.

**MARA:** We're going back to Imani.

This is intentionally uncomfortable. Race Week can continue after the
hosts' emotional ability to sell it has broken.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# 19. Condition lanes are not one-way

Day 1 can recover.

A bad early period followed by effective intervention and hours of
ordinary racing should gradually change the show.

Do not instantly reset Mara and Jules to cheerful baseline after one
clean race.

Likewise, do not permanently poison the entire Week because of one bad
hour.

Possible Day-1 trajectories include:

-   Expected → Expected
-   Expected → Slightly Below → Expected
-   Expected → Slightly Below → Subpar
-   Expected → Subpar → Bad
-   Expected → Bad → recovering Subpar
-   Expected → Cataclysmic
-   Expected → Slightly Below → Bad → stabilized
-   Expected all day

The trajectory itself creates memory.

A recovered Day 1 is narratively different from a Day 1 that was never
troubled even if both end at the same current safety state.

This suggests retaining editorial history such as:

-   worst lane reached today
-   time since lane change
-   time since last severe event
-   whether organizers have responded
-   whether the response appears to have been followed by improvement
-   whether the hosts have already discussed the incident
-   whether the current race revisits an affected place

These are selection aids, not player-facing meters.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# 20. Day-1 race coverage after the opening five

After the first handful of historically special races, do not author
Race 6, Race 7, Race 8 ... Race 150 separately.

Author **race-situation families crossed with the Day-1 condition
lane**.

Core families:

-   ordinary pre-race
-   notable field
-   new series/discipline first appearance
-   favorite on grid
-   interesting machine
-   qualifying surprise
-   ordinary finish
-   close finish
-   dominant win
-   upset
-   penalty
-   DNF
-   mechanical retirement
-   contact
-   severe incident
-   attributed quickhack
-   unknown-cause failure
-   player enters
-   player podium
-   player wins
-   player DNF
-   standings-relevant result
-   series/championship-relevant result
-   return to previously troubled location
-   first race after official intervention
-   first race after long quiet period
-   stacked simultaneous events
-   quiet-period return to racing

Each family eventually gets whole authored variants for the condition
lanes where that distinction matters.

Not every family needs five variants. If the condition state would not
naturally affect what the hosts say, reuse the ordinary sporting beat.

**The condition lane earns its way into the conversation.**

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# 21. Day-1 temporal texture beyond races

Race results cannot carry the entire show.

As Day 1 progresses, inter-race conversation should increasingly draw
from accumulated state.

## Early Day 1

-   first winners
-   enormous variety of entrants
-   learning names
-   explaining disciplines
-   first standings
-   first team stories
-   first market/economic movement
-   first technical surprises
-   hosts' pre-Week predictions being tested
-   excitement about how much racing is still ahead

## Mid Day 1

-   series begin developing their own stories
-   some names recur naturally
-   machines acquire reputations
-   standings become worth arguing over
-   organizers may make first policy/infrastructure responses
-   Moody events begin giving the day a distinct shape
-   money, repairs, entries, withdrawals, and R&D have more history to
    discuss
-   the hosts can compare morning expectations with afternoon reality

## Late Day 1

If the Week is healthy: - exhaustion mixed with exhilaration - "I cannot
believe that was only Day 1" - evening/night racing - day's best
performances - arguments about driver/machine of the day - tomorrow
previews - standings recap - favorite moments - what surprised each
host - crews repairing overnight - sense that the festival is now fully
alive

If the Week is troubled: - unresolved incidents - official response -
what changes overnight - whether troubled locations return tomorrow -
concern without knowledge of what Day 2 will bring - cautious hope that
the organizers can correct course

If catastrophic: - Day-1 recap may barely resemble sports radio -
continuation itself can be the story - do not promise Day 2 will happen
unless the schedule/official state says it will - no meta knowledge that
the Week "usually" deteriorates

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# 22. Day-2 note for later authoring

Do not implement this section as Day-1 dialogue.

A known Race Week policy pattern may involve Day 2 allowing greater
speed or otherwise loosening limits before later restriction. When
authoring Day 2, treat the actual published policy change as an in-world
decision with competing interpretations.

Mara and Jules must not describe hidden curve machinery.

If the policy rationale is explicitly published, they can discuss it. If
not, they can argue about what they think it means while clearly
presenting that as opinion.

Return to this during the Day-2 authoring pass.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# 23. Script Bank --- Day 1 Broad Coverage

The following are **first-pass authored scenes**, not final
variance-complete production copy. They exist to give the show breadth.
Later passes can add alternate versions, tighten unsupported slots, and
cross selected scenes with condition/history.

## D1 --- Morning: "There are too many names"

**MARA:** I have a problem.

**JULES:** Already?

**MARA:** Too many drivers.

**JULES:** That's your problem?

**MARA:** I thought I knew the field. I did not know the field. I knew
*some fields*. I have three pages of names here and every time Tommy
sends another grid I find twelve more people I've never talked about.

**JULES:** Good.

**MARA:** You keep saying good to things that make my job harder.

**JULES:** A racing city should be bigger than two broadcasters.

**MARA:** That's annoyingly nice.

**JULES:** Thank you.

**MARA:** And it does feel big. That's the thing. `{DRIVER}` wins one
race, then we jump across the city and there's an entirely different
group of people doing an entirely different thing in entirely different
machinery. Then another grid. Then another.

**JULES:** Nobody gets to be the main character by default.

**MARA:** Not yet.

**JULES:** Not ever, necessarily.

**MARA:** We'll see.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## D1 --- Morning: "What are you driving?"

**MARA:** I think one of my favorite things so far is just seeing what
people brought.

**JULES:** Yes.

**MARA:** That's the whole thought?

**JULES:** It's a good thought.

**MARA:** We've got purpose-built stuff, street cars, things somebody
has clearly spent an irresponsible amount of money on, things somebody
has clearly spent an irresponsible amount of *time* on...

**JULES:** Better currency.

**MARA:** You would say that.

**JULES:** Money can buy a component. Time tells you why it doesn't fit.

**MARA:** And then somebody arrives in `{MACHINE}` and suddenly
everybody has to take it seriously because it just did
`{SUPPORTED_RESULT_OR_PERFORMANCE}`.

**JULES:** That's the part I like. The stopwatch has no respect for
price.

**MARA:** Put that on a shirt.

**JULES:** No.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## D1 --- Morning: "The city is the circuit"

**MARA:** There is something completely ridiculous about looking at a
place you've driven through a thousand times and suddenly there's a
timing gate there.

**JULES:** Changes the geometry.

**MARA:** The geometry did not change.

**JULES:** Your geometry changed.

**MARA:** Explain that sentence.

**JULES:** You used to see an intersection. Now you see braking
distance, sightline, exit width, surface change.

**MARA:** Okay. Yeah.

**JULES:** A hill stops being a hill. It's where the car loses speed. A
long road stops being a commute. It's gearing.

**MARA:** And somewhere in Night City right now, somebody is discovering
that the shortcut they were very proud of is a terrible racing line.

**JULES:** Probably several people.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## D1 --- First meaningful standings discussion

**MARA:** All right, I think the standings are officially old enough for
me to care about them.

**JULES:** They were valid before.

**MARA:** Valid and interesting are different things.

**JULES:** Fair.

**MARA:** `{LEADER}` at the top of `{SERIES_OR_RELEVANT_BOARD}`. `{P2}`
behind. `{P3}` right there. And the thing I like is none of those
numbers are theoretical anymore. Every point has a race attached to it.

**JULES:** That's when a board gets useful. You can trace it backward.

**MARA:** `{DRIVER}` is there because of `{SUPPORTED_RESULT}`.
`{OTHER_DRIVER}` lost ground because of `{SUPPORTED_RESULT}`. It starts
turning into memory.

**JULES:** And eventually pressure.

**MARA:** Not yet?

**JULES:** Ask them.

**MARA:** I would love to.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## D1 --- A close finish

**TOMMY:** `{WINNER}` gets it! `{MARGIN}` over `{P2}`!

**MARA:** Oh, come on!

**JULES:** That's close.

**MARA:** That's *rude*. You do all that racing and somebody takes it
from you by `{MARGIN}`.

**JULES:** Or you take it from them.

**MARA:** Depends which microphone you're holding.

**TOMMY:** I don't think either of them knew until the line.

**MARA:** That's the good stuff. `{WINNER}` takes `{EVENT_NAME}`.
Somebody find `{P2}` a chair.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## D1 --- A dominant win

**MARA:** That wasn't close.

**JULES:** No.

**MARA:** `{WINNER}` by `{MARGIN}`.

**JULES:** And now the useful question is why.

**MARA:** You may ask the question. You may not invent the answer.

**JULES:** Thank you, Mara.

**MARA:** I've met you.

**JULES:** We know `{SUPPORTED_PERFORMANCE_FACT}`. We know
`{SUPPORTED_MACHINE_OR_GRID_FACT}`. That's enough to make the next
appearance interesting.

**MARA:** Because now everybody watches.

**JULES:** Exactly.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## D1 --- Mechanical retirement

**MARA:** Ah, no. `{DRIVER}` is out.

**JULES:** `{SUPPORTED_FAILURE_OR_DNF_REASON}`.

**MARA:** They were running `{POSITION_IF_KNOWN}`.

**JULES:** That's racing.

**MARA:** I know. Doesn't make it hurt less.

**JULES:** Especially if the pace was there.

**MARA:** And now the garage gets a race of its own.

**JULES:** That's always true.

**MARA:** Can they fix it before the next thing they care about?

**JULES:** Now you're asking the right question.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## D1 --- Penalty argument

**MARA:** I don't like that.

**JULES:** The penalty or the rule?

**MARA:** The penalty.

**JULES:** Those are different complaints.

**MARA:** I know. I understand the rule.

**JULES:** Then what's the issue?

**MARA:** `{DRIVER}` loses `{SUPPORTED_POSITION_OR_TIME}` for
`{SUPPORTED_INFRACTION}`. I understand it. I still hate watching a
result change after the finish.

**JULES:** That's why you enforce the rule before everybody learns it
isn't a rule.

**MARA:** I knew you'd be unbearable about this.

**JULES:** Consistency is not unbearable.

**MARA:** You make it look easy.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## D1 --- First quickhack that matters to a result

**MARA:** Okay, that changed the race.

**JULES:** It did.

**MARA:** `{SUPPORTED_HACK_EVENT}` on `{TARGET}`. And because this is
Race Week in Night City, apparently we have to discuss cyberwarfare as
racecraft now.

**JULES:** If it's permitted in this event, yes.

**MARA:** Do you like it?

**JULES:** That's a different question.

**MARA:** I'm asking that one.

**JULES:** I like that a driver has more to defend than a racing line.

**MARA:** That sounded suspiciously like yes.

**JULES:** I also like brakes. I don't like losing them.

**MARA:** There we go.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## D1 --- Successful quickhack defense

**MARA:** Did that fail?

**JULES:** Looks like `{TARGET}` defended it.

**MARA:** I love that.

**JULES:** Of course you do.

**MARA:** No, because everybody talks about the attack. Somebody spends
all this effort getting into your systems and the answer is just, no.
Not today.

**JULES:** Defense is racecraft too.

**MARA:** Put *that* on the shirt.

**JULES:** We have too many shirts.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## D1 --- First interesting repair turnaround

**MARA:** Wait. That's the same `{MACHINE}`?

**JULES:** Same identity if the record is right.

**MARA:** They had `{SUPPORTED_PRIOR_DAMAGE_OR_FAILURE}` earlier.

**JULES:** And they're entered again.

**MARA:** See, this is what I mean. We keep talking about drivers, but
there are machines having a Week too.

**JULES:** Absolutely.

**MARA:** They get hurt, fixed, changed, sold, brought back out. By
tonight we're gonna recognize some of these cars before we recognize
who's driving them.

**JULES:** You will.

**MARA:** You already do.

**JULES:** Yes.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## D1 --- Money starts becoming real

**CELESTE STINGER**

**CELESTE:** Can I ruin everybody's romantic interpretation of the
morning?

**MARA:** Please.

**CELESTE:** `{SUPPORTED_PAYOUT_OR_MARKET_FACT}`.

**MARA:** That's real money.

**CELESTE:** Racing tends to become much more philosophically important
once somebody gets paid.

**JULES:** How much movement are you seeing?

**CELESTE:** What I can actually confirm is `{SUPPORTED_ECONOMIC_FACT}`.
I'm not going to tell you somebody's broke because they bought a
gearbox.

**MARA:** Thank you.

**CELESTE:** But money is moving now. Results have prices attached.
Machines have prices attached. Decisions that sounded sporting at noon
are going to sound financial by dinner.

**MARA:** You're enjoying this.

**CELESTE:** Immensely.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## D1 --- Midday: "This is actually happening everywhere"

**MARA:** I had this stupid idea before today that there would be
moments where Race Week felt like one event.

**JULES:** And?

**MARA:** It doesn't. It feels like fifty things happening at once.

**JULES:** That's because it is.

**MARA:** We finish talking about `{EVENT_A}` and `{EVENT_B}` is already
staging. Imani has something from `{PUBLIC_STORY}`. Nia wants to talk
about `{MACHINE_OR_RND_TOPIC}`. Celeste is following money. Tommy has
apparently forgotten studios exist.

**JULES:** He's happier that way.

**MARA:** And there are races we're not talking about at all.

**JULES:** There have to be.

**MARA:** I like that. The Week is bigger than the show.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## D1 --- Quiet period

**MARA:** This is weird.

**JULES:** What?

**MARA:** I can hear the studio.

**JULES:** You've been able to hear it all day.

**MARA:** No, I mean nothing is starting in the next thirty seconds.

**JULES:** Ah.

**MARA:** I forgot this job could have silence.

**JULES:** Don't frighten it.

\[Pause.\]

**MARA:** So.

**JULES:** So.

**MARA:** Tires.

**JULES:** I knew you'd come around.

This can naturally lead into a long technical conversation rather than
requiring another race trigger.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## D1 --- Garage long-form: tires

**NIA STINGER**

**NIA:** You called?

**MARA:** Jules threatened to talk about tires.

**NIA:** Then you need adult supervision.

**JULES:** Betrayal.

**MARA:** Nia, we've had enough racing now that people are starting to
ask why the same machine can look completely different from one event to
another.

**NIA:** Because "the same machine" is doing a lot of work in that
sentence.

**MARA:** Go on.

**NIA:** Temperature, surface, load, setup, tire state, driver, route.
People like one-number explanations because they're portable. "This car
has grip." Fine. When? Where? For how long? Under what load?

**JULES:** Thank you.

**NIA:** Don't thank me yet. Mechanics do the same thing with engines.

**MARA:** Excellent. Fight.

**NIA:** The useful thing Race Week gives us is repetition under
changing conditions. Not identical tests. Evidence.

**MARA:** Jules has infected you with that word.

**NIA:** Jules stole it from engineering.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

**JULES:** That's generally how learning works.

**MARA:** Thank you, Jules. What I'm saying is I can see `{DRIVER}` and
remember `{SUPPORTED_EARLIER_WEEK_FACT}`. I can see `{MACHINE}` and know
it had `{SUPPORTED_MACHINE_HISTORY}`. The Week has continuity now.

**JULES:** That's why records matter.

**MARA:** Don't make this about databases.

**JULES:** Everything is about databases eventually.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## D4+ --- Healthy: seven days starts feeling plausible

**MARA:** You know what I haven't asked in a while?

**JULES:** Whether we'll make it seven days.

**MARA:** Yeah.

**JULES:** That's probably a good sign.

**MARA:** It is.

Do not use this merely because the calendar reached Day 4. It requires a
genuinely sustainable Week.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## D4+ --- Troubled: normalization becomes morally uncomfortable

**MARA:** Something happened to me today that I didn't like.

**JULES:** What?

**MARA:** `{SUPPORTED_TROUBLING_EVENT}` happened and my first thought
was, okay, where does that put the schedule?

**JULES:** Mm.

**MARA:** Not "is everybody okay." Schedule.

**JULES:** You've been doing this for days.

**MARA:** I know. That's why I don't like it.

**JULES:** People normalize conditions.

**MARA:** Doesn't mean I want to.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## D4+ --- Troubled: Darnell is done with them

**DARNELL:** You know what day it is?

**MARA:** Day `{DAY}`.

**DARNELL:** Wrong. It's the day I stopped caring who has pole.

**JULES:** Rough morning?

**DARNELL:** `{SUPPORTED_LOCAL_CONTEXT}`.

**MARA:** Fair.

**DARNELL:** I like racing. I need you both to understand that before
you make me the anti-racing guy.

**MARA:** I wasn't going to.

**DARNELL:** Good. I like racing. I also like crossing a street.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## D5+ --- The show is exhausted

**MARA:** Welcome back to Race Week Live. I'm Mara Velez.

**JULES:** Julian Mercer.

**MARA:** I almost said your name.

**JULES:** I noticed.

**MARA:** Day `{DAY}`.

**JULES:** How much sleep?

**MARA:** Don't ask questions you don't want answered.

**JULES:** Fair.

**MARA:** `{NEXT_EVENT}` coming up. I have no transition.

**JULES:** That was the transition.

**MARA:** Excellent. We're professionals.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## D5+ --- A late new winner

**MARA:** I love this.

**JULES:** `{DRIVER}`.

**MARA:** Day `{DAY}` and Race Week still has somebody new to give us.
First win of the Week for `{DRIVER}`.

**JULES:** `{SUPPORTED_CONTEXT}`.

**MARA:** Imagine waiting this long.

**JULES:** Worth it now.

**MARA:** Absolutely.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## D5+ --- A machine returns after days absent

**MARA:** No way.

**JULES:** What?

**MARA:** `{MACHINE}`.

**JULES:** Huh.

**MARA:** We haven't seen that since `{SUPPORTED_LAST_APPEARANCE}`.

**JULES:** And now it's entered in `{EVENT}`.

**MARA:** See, this is why I don't throw anything out anymore. Race Week
keeps bringing old sentences back.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## D6/D7 --- Healthy: "This actually worked"

Only when the Week has genuinely sustained itself.

**MARA:** Can I say it yet?

**JULES:** Depends what "it" is.

**MARA:** This worked.

**JULES:** Race Week isn't over.

**MARA:** Jules.

**JULES:** Fine.

**MARA:** Look at this. Day `{DAY}`. We have
`{SUPPORTED_SCALE_OR_HISTORY}`. We have championships still alive. We
have machines that have been rebuilt three times. We have people whose
names meant nothing to us on Day One and now I'm arguing with you about
their points before breakfast.

**JULES:** That part is true.

**MARA:** Night City actually did it.

**JULES:** Is doing it.

**MARA:** I will accept the tense correction.

This should feel earned and should never appear in a badly deteriorated
Week.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## D6/D7 --- Troubled: "How much longer?"

**MARA:** I remember thinking seven days sounded exciting.

**JULES:** It was exciting.

**MARA:** Past tense?

**JULES:** I didn't mean it that way.

**MARA:** I know. I'm just tired.

**JULES:** Everybody is.

**MARA:** We know we're at `{SUPPORTED_STATUS}`. We know
`{SUPPORTED_CONTEXT}`. And there are still races on this calendar.

**JULES:** There are.

**MARA:** So we keep covering them.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## D7 --- Final morning, healthy

**MARA:** Last day.

**JULES:** Last scheduled day.

**MARA:** Do not lawyer the finale.

**JULES:** Sorry.

**MARA:** Seven days ago we sat here with no winners, no standings worth
reading, no idea which names we'd be sick of saying.

**JULES:** I am not sick of any names.

**MARA:** Liar.

**JULES:** Professional.

**MARA:** Today we finish it.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## D7 --- Final race approach

**MARA:** This is strange.

**JULES:** What?

**MARA:** First race, I couldn't stop thinking about the fact that it
was first.

**JULES:** And now?

**MARA:** I can't stop thinking about the fact that this one's last.

**JULES:** `{SUPPORTED_STAKES}`.

**MARA:** Seven days of results behind it. One race left in front of us.

**JULES:** Ready?

**MARA:** No.

**JULES:** Good.

**MARA:** Tommy, take us down.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

## D7 --- Final race finish, healthy

**TOMMY:** `{WINNER}` wins `{FINAL_EVENT}`!

\[Let the finish breathe.\]

**MARA:** That's Race Week.

**JULES:** That's Race Week.

**MARA:** `{SUPPORTED_FINAL_RESULT_CONTEXT}`.

**JULES:** `{SUPPORTED_CHAMPIONSHIP_CONTEXT}`.

**MARA:** We're gonna have a lot to say.

**JULES:** Later.

**MARA:** Yeah. Give it a second.

\[Music/trackside bed.\]

The end deserves silence before recap.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# 27. Recurring Segment Seeds

These can be expanded into dozens of long-form conversations.

## "Jules Was Wrong"

Mara keeps a literal or figurative list of predictions Jules made that
failed.

It only uses predictions actually spoken earlier by the show.

Eventually Jules begins keeping "Mara Was Wrong."

## "Machine of the Morning / Afternoon / Night"

Not a formal award unless NCM makes it one. An editorial argument based
on supported observed results.

## "Who Are You Buying?"

Mara asks Jules which competitor he believes in within a specific
series/context. Later callbacks become deterministic.

## "One Thing You Missed"

Each host brings one supported result/story the other has not discussed
on air yet. Useful for surfacing the huge field without pretending
complete coverage.

## "Garage Question"

Nia receives one bounded technical question from observed/public facts
and explicitly separates known from unknown.

## "Money Is Racing"

Celeste explains one economic event without inventing private finances.

## "Darnell's Five Minutes"

A local, personal ground-level view of one supported location/state.
Darnell never becomes a universal public-opinion oracle.

## "Tommy Has Lost His Hearing"

Recurring light bit around trackside assignment. Do not overuse.

## "The Board"

A standings-focused segment whose emotional meaning changes by day: - D1
curiosity - D2 pressure - D3 consequence - D4+ championship arithmetic -
final day stakes

## "Tomorrow"

Late-day schedule preview. On troubled Weeks it may become discussion of
whether scheduled events remain plausible, but never contradict official
state.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# 28. Moody Beat Seeds

Every actual Moody event can eventually receive its own authored family.
Generic structures:

## Moody: exciting/expansive

**MARA:** Oh, that's fun.

**JULES:** `{PUBLISHED_CHANGE}`.

**MARA:** That changes `{DIRECT_EFFECT}`.

**JULES:** Immediately.

**MARA:** Okay. I want to see who notices first.

## Moody: restrictive

**MARA:** There it is.

**JULES:** `{PUBLISHED_CHANGE}`.

**MARA:** After `{SUPPORTED_CONTEXT}`, I can't say I'm surprised.

**JULES:** Surprised and justified are separate questions.

**MARA:** And you're about to make me have both conversations.

## Moody: economically significant

**CELESTE:** Stop looking at the track for a second.

**MARA:** Dangerous request.

**CELESTE:** `{PUBLISHED_CHANGE}` just changed
`{SUPPORTED_ECONOMIC_EFFECT}`.

**JULES:** How quickly does that matter?

**CELESTE:** Depends who was already committed.

## Moody: weird but not dangerous

**MARA:** I love Race Week.

**JULES:** Because?

**MARA:** Because somebody just officially decided `{PUBLISHED_CHANGE}`
and now hundreds of people have to incorporate that into their day.

**JULES:** That's governance.

**MARA:** Don't ruin it.

# 29. First Flight Evidence --- Do Not Rush It

These beats belong only when the player/world actually demonstrates the
relevant capability.

## Suspicious airborne event

**NIA:** Can I ask you two something?

**MARA:** Always dangerous when you start like that.

**NIA:** Did either of you see `{MACHINE}` at `{LOCATION}`?

**JULES:** The jump?

**NIA:** Was it?

**MARA:** What else would it be?

**NIA:** I don't know yet.

**JULES:** How long?

**NIA:** `{SUPPORTED_AIRBORNE_DURATION_OR_OBSERVATION}`.

\[Pause.\]

**MARA:** That's a long jump.

**NIA:** That's why I'm asking.

Then move on. No unlock language.

## Repeated credible flight evidence

**MARA:** Okay, you don't get to call that a jump anymore.

**JULES:** I wasn't going to.

**NIA:** `{SUPPORTED_FLIGHT_FACT}`.

**MARA:** So what do we call it?

**NIA:** Right now? Something that stayed in the air and remained
controlled for `{SUPPORTED_FACT}`.

**MARA:** Nia.

**NIA:** You want me to say "flying."

**MARA:** A little.

**NIA:** Earn it again.

------------------------------------------------------------------------

# 30. First Deep Rupture --- Working Long Scene

This remains provisional until direct-address policy is finalized.

The player crosses Deep after the show has already acknowledged
meaningful flight. The system warnings have ended. The radio should
allow silence first.

\[Long silence / altered reception / sparse bed as appropriate.\]

**MARA:** ...Jules?

**JULES:** I'm here.

**MARA:** Are we still connected to `{PLAYER_NAME_OR_DRIVER_REFERENCE}`?

**JULES:** Apparently.

**MARA:** How?

**JULES:** I don't know.

\[Pause.\]

**MARA:** Nia?

**NIA:** I am looking at the same thing you are.

**MARA:** Which is?

**NIA:** `{SUPPORTED_TELEMETRY_FACT}`. Beyond that, I'm not giving you a
story I can't prove.

\[Pause.\]

**MARA:** Can they hear us?

**JULES:** I don't know.

**MARA:** You said we're connected.

**JULES:** Receiving and hearing are not the same thing.

**MARA:** Right.

\[Pause.\]

**MARA:** Okay. This is going to sound insane.

\[Pause.\]

**MARA:** You. `{PLAYER_NAME}`. If you can hear me, yes, I'm talking to
you.

**JULES:** Mara.

**MARA:** What? We're past normal.

\[Pause.\]

**MARA:** We can still see `{SUPPORTED_TELEMETRY_FACT}`. You're outside
`{SUPPORTED_MAP_OR_KNOWN_BOUNDARY_LANGUAGE}`. We do not know what you're
looking at. We do not know what happens next.

**JULES:** And before anybody says congratulations...

**MARA:** Yeah.

**JULES:** Come back.

**MARA:** Please come back.

Do not explain Deep. Do not name external destinations. Do not convert
discovery into tutorial copy.
